href="#cite_note-Freudenberg2008-255"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>253<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>254<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Challenges">Challenges</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Criticism_of_science" title="Criticism of science">Criticism of science</a> and <a href="/wiki/Academic_bias" title="Academic bias">Academic bias</a></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Replication_crisis" title="Replication crisis">replication crisis</a> is an ongoing <a href="/wiki/Methodological" class="mw-redirect" title="Methodological">methodological</a> crisis that affects parts of the <a href="/wiki/Social_science" title="Social science">social</a> and <a href="/wiki/Life_science" class="mw-redirect" title="Life science">life sciences</a>. In subsequent investigations, the results of many scientific studies have been proven to be <a href="/wiki/Reproducibility" title="Reproducibility">unrepeatable</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>255<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The crisis has long-standing roots; the phrase was coined in the early 2010s<sup id="cite&#95;ref-258" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-258"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>256<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> as part of a growing awareness of the problem. The replication crisis represents an important body of research in <a href="/wiki/Metascience" title="Metascience">metascience</a>, which aims to improve the quality of all scientific research while reducing waste.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-259" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-259"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>257<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The term <a href="/wiki/Scientific_misconduct" title="Scientific misconduct">scientific misconduct</a> refers to situations such as where researchers have intentionally misrepresented their published data or have purposely given credit for a discovery to the wrong person.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-260" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-260"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>258<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
An area of study or speculation that masquerades as science in an attempt to claim legitimacy that it would not otherwise be able to achieve is sometimes referred to as <a href="/wiki/Pseudoscience" title="Pseudoscience">pseudoscience</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fringe_science" title="Fringe science">fringe science</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Junk_science" title="Junk science">junk science</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-261" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-261"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>259<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-262" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-262"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>260<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Physicist <a href="/wiki/Richard_Feynman" title="Richard Feynman">Richard Feynman</a> coined the term "<a href="/wiki/Cargo_cult_science" class="mw-redirect" title="Cargo cult science">cargo cult science</a>" for cases in which researchers believe, and at a glance, look like they are doing science but lack the honesty to allow their results to be rigorously evaluated.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-263" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-263"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>261<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Various types of commercial advertising, ranging from hype to fraud, may fall into these categories. Science has been described as "the most important tool" for separating valid claims from invalid ones.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>262<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Sometimes, research can be well-intended but is incorrect, obsolete, incomplete, or over-simplified expositions of scientific ideas.
</p><p>There can also be an element of <a href="/wiki/Political_bias" title="Political bias">political bias</a> or ideological bias in science. Scientists in some countries were found to have a bias in <a href="/wiki/Political_party" title="Political party">political party</a> preferences compared to the general population.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-y499&#95;265-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-y499-265"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>263<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>

<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references" data-mw-group="lower-alpha">
<li id="cite&#95;note-doubtPtolemy-81"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-doubtPtolemy_81-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Ibn al-Haytham's <i><a href="/wiki/Book_of_Optics" title="Book of Optics">Book of Optics</a></i> disputed Claudius Ptolemy's extramission theory of vision: "Hence, the extramission of [visual] rays is superfluous and useless". See A. Mark Smith's translation from the Latin,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Smith2001&#95;80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Smith2001-80"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>80<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Book I, [6.54], pp. 372, 408.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite&#95;note-HelmholtzGibbsGuthLinde-124"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-HelmholtzGibbsGuthLinde_124-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Whether the universe is closed or open, or the <a href="/wiki/Shape_of_the_universe" title="Shape of the universe">shape of the universe</a>, is an open question. The 2nd law of thermodynamics,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Reichl1980&#95;121-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Reichl1980-121"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>120<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 9">&#58;&#8202;9&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>121<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and the 3rd law of thermodynamics<sup id="cite&#95;ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>122<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> imply the <a href="/wiki/Heat_death_of_the_universe" title="Heat death of the universe">heat death of the universe</a> if the universe is a closed system, but not necessarily for an expanding universe.</span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>

<li id="cite&#95;note-20"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-20">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><cite id="CITEREFLightman2011" class="citation book cs1">Lightman, Bernard (2011). "13. Science and the Public". In Shank, Michael; Numbers, Ronald; Harrison, Peter (eds.). <i>Wrestling with Nature: From Omens to Science</i>. University of Chicago Press. p.&#160;367. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-226-31783-0" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-226-31783-0"><bdi>978-0-226-31783-0</bdi></a>.</cite><span title="ctx&#95;ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft&#95;val&#95;fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=13.+Science+and+the+Public&amp;rft.btitle=Wrestling+with+Nature%3A+From+Omens+to+Science&amp;rft.pages=367&amp;rft.pub=University+of+Chicago+Press&amp;rft.date=2011&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-226-31783-0&amp;rft.aulast=Lightman&amp;rft.aufirst=Bernard&amp;rfr&#95;id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AScience" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite&#95;note-21"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-21">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><cite id="CITEREFHarrison2015" class="citation book cs1"><a href="/wiki/Peter_Harrison_(historian)" title="Peter Harrison (historian)">Harrison, Peter</a> (2015). <i>The Territories of Science and Religion</i>. University of Chicago Press. pp.&#160;<span class="nowrap">164–</span>165. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-226-18451-7" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-226-18451-7"><bdi>978-0-226-18451-7</bdi></a>. <q>The changing character of those engaged in scientific endeavors was matched by a new nomenclature for their endeavors. The most conspicuous marker of this change was the replacement of "natural philosophy" by "natural science". In 1800 few had spoken of the "natural sciences" but by 1880 this expression had overtaken the traditional label "natural philosophy". The persistence of "natural philosophy" in the twentieth century is owing largely to historical references to a past practice (see figure 11). As should now be apparent, this was not simply the substitution of one term by another, but involved the jettisoning of a range of personal qualities relating to the conduct of philosophy and the living of the philosophical life.</q></cite><span title="ctx&#95;ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft&#95;val&#95;fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Territories+of+Science+and+Religion&amp;rft.pages=164-165&amp;rft.pub=University+of+Chicago+Press&amp;rft.date=2015&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-226-18451-7&amp;rft.aulast=Harrison&amp;rft.aufirst=Peter&amp;rfr&#95;id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AScience" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>

<li id="cite&#95;note-53"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-53">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">An account of the pre-Socratic use of the concept of φύσις may be found in <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><cite id="CITEREFNaddaf2006" class="citation book cs1">Naddaf, Gerard (2006). <i>The Greek Concept of Nature</i>. SUNY Press,</cite><span title="ctx&#95;ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft&#95;val&#95;fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Greek+Concept+of+Nature&amp;rft.pub=SUNY+Press&amp;rft.date=2006&amp;rft.aulast=Naddaf&amp;rft.aufirst=Gerard&amp;rfr&#95;id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AScience" class="Z3988"></span> and in <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><cite id="CITEREFDucarmeCouvet2020" class="citation journal cs1">Ducarme, Frédéric; Couvet, Denis (2020). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://hal.science/hal-02554932/file/s41599-020-0390-y.pdf">"What does 'nature' mean?"</a> <span class="cs1-format">(PDF)</span>. <i><a href="/wiki/Palgrave_Communications" class="mw-redirect" title="Palgrave Communications">Palgrave Communications</a></i>. <b>6</b> (14) 14. <a href="/wiki/Springer_Nature" title="Springer Nature">Springer Nature</a>. <a href="/wiki/Doi_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="Doi (identifier)">doi</a>:<span class="id-lock-free" title="Freely accessible"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://doi.org/10.1057%2Fs41599-020-0390-y">10.1057/s41599-020-0390-y</a></span>. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20230816053756/https://hal.science/hal-02554932/file/s41599-020-0390-y.pdf">Archived</a> <span class="cs1-format">(PDF)</span> from the original on 16 August 2023<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">16 August</span> 2023</span>.</cite><span title="ctx&#95;ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft&#95;val&#95;fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=Palgrave+Communications&amp;rft.atitle=What+does+%27nature%27+mean%3F&amp;rft.volume=6&amp;rft.issue=14&amp;rft.artnum=14&amp;rft.date=2020&amp;rft&#95;id=info%3Adoi%2F10.1057%2Fs41599-020-0390-y&amp;rft.aulast=Ducarme&amp;rft.aufirst=Fr%C3%A9d%C3%A9ric&amp;rft.au=Couvet%2C+Denis&amp;rft&#95;id=https%3A%2F%2Fhal.science%2Fhal-02554932%2Ffile%2Fs41599-020-0390-y.pdf&amp;rfr&#95;id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AScience" class="Z3988"></span> The word φύσις, while first used in connection with a plant in Homer, occurs early in Greek philosophy, and in several senses. Generally, these senses match rather well the current senses in which the English word <i>nature</i> is used, as confirmed by <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><cite id="CITEREFGuthrie" class="citation book cs1">Guthrie, W. K. C. <i>Presocratic Tradition from Parmenides to Democritus</i></cite><span title="ctx&#95;ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft&#95;val&#95;fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Presocratic+Tradition+from+Parmenides+to+Democritus&amp;rft.aulast=Guthrie&amp;rft.aufirst=W.+K.+C.&amp;rfr&#95;id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AScience" class="Z3988"></span> (volume 2 of his <i>History of Greek Philosophy</i>), Cambridge University Press, 1965.</span>
</li>

<li id="cite&#95;note-Smith1981-88"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-Smith1981_88-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Smith1981_88-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><cite id="CITEREFSmith1981" class="citation journal cs1">Smith, A. Mark (1981). "Getting the Big Picture in Perspectivist Optics". <i>Isis</i>. <b>72</b> (4): <span class="nowrap">568–</span>589. <a href="/wiki/Doi_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="Doi (identifier)">doi</a>:<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://doi.org/10.1086%2F352843">10.1086/352843</a>. <a href="/wiki/JSTOR_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="JSTOR (identifier)">JSTOR</a>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/stable/231249">231249</a>. <a href="/wiki/PMID_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="PMID (identifier)">PMID</a>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7040292">7040292</a>. <a href="/wiki/S2CID_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="S2CID (identifier)">S2CID</a>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID:27806323">27806323</a>.</cite><span title="ctx&#95;ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft&#95;val&#95;fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=Isis&amp;rft.atitle=Getting+the+Big+Picture+in+Perspectivist+Optics&amp;rft.volume=72&amp;rft.issue=4&amp;rft.pages=568-589&amp;rft.date=1981&amp;rft&#95;id=info%3Apmid%2F7040292&amp;rft&#95;id=https%3A%2F%2Fapi.semanticscholar.org%2FCorpusID%3A27806323%23id-name%3DS2CID&amp;rft&#95;id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.jstor.org%2Fstable%2F231249%23id-name%3DJSTOR&amp;rft&#95;id=info%3Adoi%2F10.1086%2F352843&amp;rft.aulast=Smith&amp;rft.aufirst=A.+Mark&amp;rfr&#95;id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AScience" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite&#95;note-89"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-89">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"The man who had deposed the earth from its proud position as the centre of the universe and had recognized it to be merely one of the planets, had yet felt compelled to give it quite an exceptional position in his new system. Though he had said 'in the midst of all stand the sun,' he had in his planetary theories assumed the centre of all movements to be the centre of the earth's orbit, where the sun was not." Dreyer, J.L.E. <i>A History of Astronomy from Thales to Kepler,</i> (Dover Publications, 1953). p.343. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/9780486600796" title="Special:BookSources/9780486600796"><bdi>9780486600796</bdi></a></span>
</li>
<li id="cite&#95;note-90"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-90">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"...it was obvious to Copernicus...that the earth cannot move uniformly about a circle with the sun at the center. Thus Copernicus placed the sun not at the center of the earth's orbit, but at some distance away. The center of the solar system, and of the universe, in the system of Copernicus is thus not the sun at all, but rather a 'mean sun,' of the center of the earth's orbit. Hence, it is preferable&#160;to call the Copernican system a heliostatic system rather than a heliocentric system." <a href="/wiki/I._Bernard_Cohen" title="I. Bernard Cohen">Cohen, I. Bernard</a>. <i>The Birth of a New Physics (Revised and Updated)</i> (W.W. Norton &amp; Company, 1985). p.44. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-393-01994-2" title="Special:BookSources/0-393-01994-2"><bdi>0-393-01994-2</bdi></a></span>
</li>
<li id="cite&#95;note-91"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-91">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"All movement within it (outside of the immediate vicinity of the Earth) is determined by the fundamental principle that heavenly movement is circular and therefore unchanging. Ptolemy, Copernicus thought, had betrayed this principle not...by adding epicycles to deferents in order to explain why the planets sometimes appear to move backwards in the sky, but by introducing the <a href="/wiki/Equant" title="Equant">equant</a> in order to speed them up and slow them down." <a href="/wiki/David_Wootton_(historian)" title="David Wootton (historian)">Wootton, David</a>. <i>The Invention of Science: A New History of the Scientific Revolution</i> (Penguin, 2015). p.152. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-06-175952-X" title="Special:BookSources/0-06-175952-X"><bdi>0-06-175952-X</bdi></a></span>
</li>
<li id="cite&#95;note-92"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-92">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"My Copernican census eventually helped to establish that the majority of sixteenth-century astronomers thought eliminating the <a href="/wiki/Equant" title="Equant">equant</a> was Copernicus' big achievement, because it satisfied the ancient aesthetic principle that eternal celestial motions should be uniform and circular or compounded of uniform and circular parts." Gingerich, Owen. <i>The Book Nobody Read</i> (Walker &amp; Company, 2004).p.55. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-8027-1415-3" title="Special:BookSources/0-8027-1415-3"><bdi>0-8027-1415-3</bdi></a></span>
</li>
<li id="cite&#95;note-93"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-93">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"It is easy to show that conventional Ptolemaic astronomy was thriving until 1610 [when Galileo observed the phases of Venus with a telescope] and went into crisis immediately afterwards...The evidence is clear: <a href="/wiki/Geocentrism#Ptolemaic_model" title="Geocentrism">Ptolemaic astronomy</a> was unaffected by Copernicus; it went into crisis briefly with the <a href="/wiki/SN_1572" title="SN 1572">new star of 1572</a>, but by the end of the sixteenth century it had fully recovered. The telescope, on the other hand, brought about its immediate and irreversible collapse." <a href="/wiki/David_Wootton_(historian)" title="David Wootton (historian)">Wootton, David</a>. <i>The Invention of Science: A New History of the Scientific Revolution</i> (Penguin, 2015). p.152. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-06-175952-X" title="Special:BookSources/0-06-175952-X"><bdi>0-06-175952-X</bdi></a></span>
</li>

<li id="cite&#95;note-100"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-100">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><cite id="CITEREFGribbin2002" class="citation book cs1">Gribbin, John (2002). <i>Science: A History 1543–2001</i>.
